Run this command to create a dataset:\n    \n    `python generator\\EPYNET\\scenegenv7.py   --config <your_ini_path>\n                                            --executors <num_of_workers>\n                                            --batch_size <batch_size_per_worker>\n    `\n\nThere are many flags and options introduced in `scenegenv7.py`.\n\nThe new dataset will be a zip file (.ZIP).\n\n## Training model\n\nUser can train GATRes model using this default command:\n    \n    `python train.py --model gatres_small\n                    --epochs 500\n                    --batch_size 8\n                    --device 'cuda'\n                    --mask_rate 0.95\n                    --datasets_paths <dataset_path1> <optional_dataset_path2> ...\n                    --input_paths <inp_path1> <optional_inp_path2> ...\n                    --test_data_path <test_dataset_path>\n                    --test_input_path <test_dataset_path>\n                    --save_path <where_to_save_checkpoints>\n    `\n\n## Inference\n\nWhen you run the above command, the trained model will be tested immediately after training. Otherwise, you can make a manual test using this command:\n    \n    `python evaluation.py    --model gatres_small\n                            --model_path <where_to_load_trained_weights>\n                            --batch_size 8\n                            --num_test_trials 10\n                            --test_type 'clean'\n                            --device 'cuda'\n                            --mask_rate 0.95\n                            --datasets_paths <train_dataset_path> \n                            --input_paths <train_inp_path>\n                            --test_data_path <test_dataset_path>\n                            --test_input_path <test_dataset_path>\n    `\n\n## License\n\nMIT license. Index Terms— leak localization, dictionary learning, interpo-\nData-driven Leak Localization in Water Distribution Networks via\nDictionary Learning and Graph-based Interpolation\nPaul Irofti1, Luis Romero-Ben2, Florin Stoican1,3, Vicenc¸ Puig2\nAbstract— In this paper, we propose a data-driven leak\nlocalization method for water distribution networks (WDNs)\nwhich combines two complementary approaches: graph-based\ninterpolation and dictionary classiﬁcation. The former estimates\nthe complete WDN hydraulic state (i.e., hydraulic heads) from\nreal measurements at certain nodes and the network graph.\nThen, we append to the actual measurements a subset of\nrelevant estimated states to feed and train the dictionary\nlearning scheme. Thus, the meshing of these two methods\nis explored, and several promising performance results are\nattained, even deriving different mechanisms to increase the\nresilience to classical issues (e.g., dimensionality, interpolation\nerrors, etc.). The approach is validated using the L-TOWN\nbenchmark proposed in the BattLeDIM2020 competition.\nIndex Terms— leak localization, dictionary learning, interpo-\nproblem size. Thus, there is a recent trend in the state of the\nart to apply data-driven methods [5]. This avoids the need of\nhaving an accurate mathematical model required by model-\nbased approaches but neglects some physical relations that\nexist in the real system. A possible way to compensate the\ndisregarding of these physical relations is by exploiting the\nsystem structure, i.e., the network’s graph.\nContributions. The above led us to the idea of com-\nbining two recently developed and promising approaches:\nthe graph-state interpolation (GSI) procedure proposed in\n[6] and the dictionary learning (DL) detailed in [7]. In\nthis way, interpolated data (virtual sensors), generated in\nan unsupervised way, is added to the real measurements to\nfeed the learning step of DL, hence indirectly considering\nstructural information during the leak localization. Moreover,\nthe use of a robust classiﬁcation step [8], provided by DL,\nallows to improve the node-level classiﬁcation of GSI.\nThus, this article constitutes an initial step in the devel-\nWe brieﬂy introduce the theoretical background used in\nthe rest of the paper.\nA. Dictionary Learning Methods\nRecently, we successfully applied dictionary learning tech-\nniques to solve WDN fault detection and isolation prob-\nlems [7], [11]. The DL problem trains an over-complete base\nD ∈Rm×n, also called a dictionary, on a dataset Y ∈\nRm×N to produce the s-sparse representations X ∈Rn×N:\nmin\nD,X\n∥Y −DX∥2\nF\n(1)\nPaul Irofti and Florin Stoican were supported by grants of the Romanian\nMinistry of Education and Research, CNCS - UEFISCDI, project number\nPN-III-P1-1.1-PD-2019-0825 and project number PN-III-P2-2.1-PED-2019-\n3248, within PNCDI III. The former estimates the complete WDN hydraulic state (i.e., hydraulic heads) from real measurements at certain nodes and the network graph. Then, these actual measurements, together with a subset of valuable estimated states, are used to feed and train the dictionary learning scheme. Thus, the meshing of these two methods is explored, showing that its performance is superior to either approach alone, even deriving different mechanisms to increase its resilience to classical problems (e.g., dimensionality, interpolation errors, etc.). The approach is validated using the L-TOWN benchmark proposed at BattLeDIM2020.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n2022 IEEE Conference on Control (https://www.proceedings.com/content/066/066691webtoc.pdf)\nciteturn6search14 [wordlim: 200] Published: 7 months ago; Data-Driven Leak Localization in Water Distribution Networks Via Dictionary Learning and ... The former estimates the complete WDN hydraulic state (i.e., hydraulic heads) from real measurements at certain nodes and the network graph. Then, these actual measurements, together with a subset of valuable estimated states, are used to feed and train the dictionary learning scheme.\nL17: Thus, the meshing of these two methods is explored, showing that its performance is superior to either approach alone, even deriving different mechanisms to increase its resilience to classical problems (e.g., dimensionality, interpolation errors, etc.). Discussion\\r\\nThe BattleDIM Challenge 2020 introduced a metric [6] to evaluate leakage detection algorithms\\r\\nin water supply networks. This metric considers spatial and temporal characteristics of predicted\\r\\npipe leakages, aiming to capture their economic impact. However in our experiments, optimizing\\r\\nfor this metric seems to introduce unintended and non-intuitive artefacts as for example a\\r\\nmodel with lower TPR and higher FPs can nevertheless result in a better economic score. The\\r\\nstringent penalties for undetected or late-identified leakages may inadvertently increase false\\r\\npredictions without proportional consequences. This can impose additional costs on water\\r\\nsuppliers. Moreover, the metric only evaluates the initial prediction and does not account for\\r\\npotential improvements over subsequent time steps. This is further distorted by the hierarchical\\r\\norder of evaluation, in which the first detected time-step is taken more into account than the\\r\\nones that follow in chronological order. The optimization of the model towards a high economic\\r\\nscore comes along with an increasing number of false values, which are not ranked high for the\\r\\n\\fpenalty calculation. When we use different metrics like F1, Precision, Recall, Balanced Accuracy\\r\\nor AUC-ROC to set the threshold on the validation split the model is optimized towards a higher\\r\\nTrue Positive rate and focuses more on eliminating false predictions. The highest True Positive\\r\\nRate we gained was 82,61 percent. If we manually tune the threshold on the validation set, we\\r\\nhave been able to gain an Economic Score of 326,521 as highest value but at the cost of 138\\r\\nFalse Positives. This means, that if the model is optimized to predict as much as possible as\\r\\ntrue value the Economic Score rises, but the False values also go up. Nevertheless, the metricâ€™s\\r\\nintegration of spatial and temporal failures is commendable, catering to the specific needs of\\r\\nwater distribution networks. To ensure fair evaluations, future enhancements should strike a\\r\\nbalanced approach, considering both false predictions and missed leakages while incorporating\\r\\niterative performance improvements. By addressing these concerns, the metric could be further\\r\\nrefined to effectively capture the real-world challenges faced by leakage detection algorithms.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n8. Conclusion\\r\\nWe have gained the highest Economic Score for the Liquor-HGNN approach in comparison to the\\r\\nother challenge submissions. We demonstrated the effectiveness of the localization of leakages\\r\\nin sparsely measured water distribution networks. We further integrated Liquor-HGNN with a\\r\\ndemand prediction pre-processing model that leverages the underlying physical information of\\r\\nthe hydraulic system. This combination enabled us to overcome the challenges posed by sparse\\r\\ndata in complex sensor distribution networks.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nReferences\\r\\n [1] R. Dighade, M. Kadu, A. This study develops a spatialâ€“hydraulic coupled and interpretable machine learning\\r\\n                                         framework for zonal leakage localization under operational uncertainties. The WDN is first\\r\\n                                         partitioned into zones, followed by leakage-region prediction using a Stacking ensemble\\r\\n                                         integrating Random Forest, XGBoost, and MLP. SHAP analysis is employed to characterize\\r\\n                                         the contribution of individual monitoring nodes to regional classification. The proposed\\r\\n                                         Stacking framework achieves an overall localization accuracy of 0.9667 and a Macro-F1\\r\\n                                         score of 0.9666, with relatively stable diagnostic performance under pressure noise. Global\\r\\n                                         SHAP analysis identifies several monitoring nodes with substantial contributions to the\\r\\n                                         overall classification, while the observed misclassifications are mainly concentrated be-\\r\\n                                         tween a small number of neighboring regions with similar feature representations. A\\r\\n                                         further test on the Net3 network achieves an overall localization accuracy of 0.9812, pro-\\r\\n                                         viding additional evidence of the applicability of the framework across different network\\r\\n                                         configurations. (2024) focuses on localization with minimal sensors and achieves good accuracy (average distance of 180 m) with only three sensors, which is comparable to the localization accuracy achieved in some cases by the methodology proposed here. However, the methodology presented by the authors relies on the relocation of a mobile sensor to achieve good accuracy in localizing the leak. This can be challenging in real-world scenarios, where sensor relocation can be complex or time-consuming. Unlike these studies, this methodology combines leak detection and localization into a single integrated framework, simplifying the process and potentially reducing the time to identify and treat leaks. Furthermore, the methodology strategically narrows the leak search space by considering sensor coverage areas and correlations, leading to fewer hydraulic simulations and faster process- ing compared to methods that require extensive simulations or sensor relocation.\n\n  1. Conclusions\n\nThe paper presents a novel methodology based on multilayer network analysis for leak detection and localization in WDN that dem- onstrates promising results when applied to the benchmark problem presented in BattLeDIM. Particularly, the work bases its methodological fundamentals on graph analysis and monitoring data to detect anoma- lies in the network pressure data, being capable to indicate the presence of leaks within 15 minutes of their onset. However, it is important to highlight that the effectiveness of leak detection and localization varies depending on the nature of the leaks. Leaks with a gradual flow increase present additional challenges, as changes in data over time are smaller, resulting in delayed detection and less precise localization. Despite these limitations, the overall score achieved by the proposed methodology is competitive compared to other participants in BattLeDIM, showing its efficiency and performance in comparison to alternative approaches. Experiments on a real-world dataset are conducted and demonstrate that our model outperformed 9 baseline models in flow and pressure series prediction in WDS.\nTitle: Hybrid Attention Networks for Flow and Pressure Forecasting in Water Distribution Systems\nAuthors: Ziqing Ma, Shuming Liu, Guancheng Guo, Xipeng Yu\nDate: Mon Apr 13 09:00:26 2020\n\nMultivariate geo-sensory time series prediction is challenging because of the complex spatial and temporal correlation. In urban water distribution systems (WDS), numerous spatial-correlated sensors have been deployed to continuously collect hydraulic data. Forecasts of monitored flow and pressure time series are of vital importance for operational decision making, alerts and anomaly detection. To address this issue, we proposed a hybrid dual-stage spatial-temporal attention-based recurrent neural networks (hDS-RNN). Our model consists of two stages: a spatial attention-based encoder and a temporal attention-based decoder. Specifically, a hybrid spatial attention mechanism that employs inputs along temporal and spatial axes is proposed. Experiments on a real-world dataset are conducted and demonstrate that our model outperformed 9 baseline models in flow and pressure series prediction in WDS.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nInput-decoupled dual-scale attention network for flow field prediction in complex flows - ScienceDirect (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0957417425045191)\nciteturn9search0 [wordli…3301 tokens truncated…agement, Vol. 134, October 2021, pp. 78-88.</td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td></td>\n<td>6. In consequence, leak detection and localisation have been an active area of research for several decades due to these factors. ... Though these classes of model-based methods were able to detect leaks accurately, even for larger networks, they require complete information on the network hydraulics and continuous calibration which is seldom available for smaller utilities. ... In this study, we focus on pressure measurement-based encoder–decoder models and evaluate their leak detection performance. ... Despite the low quality of available measurement data and other systematic errors that may have occurred during manual data logging, the encoder–decoder models were able to generate sharp changes in reconstruction errors of different sensors, signalling either the onset of leak or sensor faults as shown in Figure 9.\n## HIGHLIGHTS\n\nListen\n\n  * Three variants of encoder–decoder neural networks are modelled for data-driven leak detection.\n\n  * The models were validated on a benchmark dataset and applied to a real-world WDN in Norway.\n\n  * The sequence learning capability of the long short-term memory autoencoder (LSTM-AE) showed better detection performance.\n\n  * The lower performance in real-world applications revealed limitations due to the positioning of sensors and data availability.\n\nanomaly detection, autoencoders, deep neural networks, multivariate timeseries\n\n## ABBREVIATIONS\n\nListen\n\nAE\n\n    \n\nautoencoder\n\nANN\n\n    \n\nartificial neural network\n\nCNN\n\n    \n\nconvolutional neural network\n\nCUMSUM\n\n    \n\ncumulative sum\n\nKL\n\n    \n\nKullback–Leibler\n\nLILA\n\n    \n\nLeak Identification and Localisation Algorithm\n\nLSTM-AE\n\n    \n\nlong short-term memory autoencoder\n\nMAE\n\n    \n\nmean absolute error\n\nMAPE\n\n    \n\nmean absolute percentage error\n\nMSE\n\n    \n\nmean squared error\n\nPMA\n\n    \n\npressure measurement area\n\nRNN\n\n    \n\nrecurrent neural network\n\nSPC\n\n    \n\nstatistical process control\n\nSWM\n\n    \n\nsmart water meter\n\nVAE\n\n    \n\nvariational autoencoder\n\nWDN\n\n    \n\nwater distribution network\n\nWNTR\n\n    \n\nWater Network Tool for Resilience\n\n## INTRODUCTION\n\nListen\n\nDetection and localisation of leaks in water distribution networks (WDNs) remain one of the main challenges for many water utilities across the world. Globally, around 126 billion m^{3} of potable water is lost during supply (Liemberger & Wyatt 2019) with a leakage rate of, on average, about 30% in Norway (Statistics Norway 2021). As WDNs are complex networked pipeline systems spanning several hundred kilometres in a wide geographical area, fast detection, localisation, and isolation of leaks becomes a complex task. Furthermore, due to the rapid urbanisation and deterioration due to aging of WDNs, continuous monitoring for rapid leak detection and isolation becomes even more challenging owing to changes in network structure and parameters. We highlight that the number and location of the pressure sensors and data quality play an important role in realising these methods in everyday scenarios.\n\nIn this study, three data-driven prediction models are trained using only pressure signals, and their leak detection performance is compared using a widely studied leak detection benchmark, which fulfils the aforementioned assumptions. The three deep neural network models – autoencoders (AEs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), and long short-term memory autoencoders (LSTM-AEs) – are trained in semi-supervised fashion using only pressure measurements. To explore their applicability in practice, the models were also trained and evaluated using measurements from a representative real-world WDN in Norway. First, a brief background on the deep learning models and methods for data preprocessing used is reported in the methodology section. The results and discussion on the detection performance of the three models used and practical limitations are then provided. Finally, the conclusions and future directions of the study are presented.\n\n## METHODOLOGY\n\nListen\n\nThe methodology in this study comprises three stages. In stage 1, the training data are obtained from pressure measurements during nominal system states representing (assumed) no-leak scenarios or from estimates using a hydraulic model calibrated for nominal operations. The training data are then pre-processed for subsequent neural network training and evaluation. As the availability of historical leak events for a given WDN is rarely available, semi-supervised learning based deep neural networks are used for leakage detection in this study. In this regard, in stage 2, three classes of encoder–decoder deep neural networks (AE, VAE, and LSTM-AE) are trained in a semi-supervised fashion. The encoder–decoder models are selected in an increasing level of model complexity and their performance for different types of leaks is compared. Subsequently, in stage 3, the predictions and real measurements are used for detecting the time of leak using statistical moving window techniques. \nFigure 2\n\nView largeDownload slide\n\nThree class of encoder–decoder model used for leak detection: (a) AE, (b) LSTM-AE, and (c) VAE.\n\nClose Figure 2\n\n###  Autoencoder\n\nListen\n\nAEs are a kind of unsupervised neural network that is used for efficient learning of higher dimensional features without the need for explicit data labelling (Baldi 2012). A typical AE architecture consists of two parts: an encoder (E) and a decoder (D), each containing one or more hidden layers of neurons, as shown in Figure 2(a). The encoding network (E) takes in the input x € R^{m} and transforms it into a lower-dimensional space z € R^{n}, where n<m through linear combinations of non-linear activations at hidden layers. The compressed data at latent layer z capture the most relevant features of the original data. The decoding network (D) then takes the compressed data z as input and transforms it back to the original space R^{m}, by increasing the dimensionality at each of the hidden layers. The output of AE Image can be represented as Image where Image⁠.\n\nDuring training, AEs aim to minimise the difference between the input data (x) and the reconstructed data Image⁠. MSE as a loss function, is used to train the AE model. By minimising this error, the AE learns a compressed representation that captures the most salient features of the input data. Hence, any considerable deviations in the reconstruction errors can be attributed to the onset of anomalous events.\n\nAEs have been widely used in classification and regression tasks across several domains. For example, AEs have been used for monitoring pipeline leakage (Wang et al. 2020) and for monitoring spacecraft sensor data (Sakurada & Yairi 2014). In this study, a simple AE is trained in semi-supervised fashion for the leak detection task.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nPublication: The performance of encoder–decoder neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks - B-WaterSmart (https://b-watersmart.eu/download/publication-the-performance-of-encoder-decoder-neural-networks-for-leak-detection-in-water-distribution-networks/)\nciteturn9search5 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2.2 years ago; Crawled: last week; Publication: The performance of encoder–decoder neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks ... August 2024\n  * Download\n\n# Publication: The performance of encoder–decoder neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks\n\nThis work outlines the performance of three variants of deep neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks, namely – autoencoders (AEs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), and long short-term memory autoencoders (LSTM-AEs). The multivariate pressure signals reconstructed from these models are analysed for leakage identification. The leak onset time is estimated using a fast approximation sliding window technique, which computes statistical discrepancies in prediction errors. The performance of all three variants is validated\nusing the widely studied L-Town ben hmark network. Furthermore, their feasibility for real-world application is studied by applying them to a real-world case study representing the data availability and network design often found in smaller- and medium-sized utilities in Norway. The results for the benchmark network showed that AE and LSTM-AE showed comparable detection performance for abrupt leaks with VAE performing the least. For incipient leaks, the LSTM-AE showed better detection performance with few false-positives. For the real-world dataset, the performance was significantly lower due to the quantity and quality of data available, and the contradiction of inherent requirements of data-driven models. In addition, the analysis revealed that the positioning of pressure sensors in the network is critical for the leak detection performance of these models.\n\nZimmermann2024-08-07T08:33:40+02:00--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nPressure Driven analysis of water distribution systems for preventing siphonic flow (https://eurekamag.com/research/095/335/095335485.php)\nciteturn9search6 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 2 months ago; Discussion\n\nL-Town is a well-established and widely used benchmark network for leakage detection and localization in water distribution systems. Originally developed for the BattLeDIM competition, it has since been extensively adopted as a standardized testbed for evaluating and comparing different leakage detection and localization methods. For example, during the competition, the Cheng00 team employed change-point detection and statistical analysis for leakage detection and localization [50], the D and W team used pressure sensitivity analysis to identify potentially leaking pipes [51], and the Leakbusters team combined data-driven pressure reconstruction with hydraulic simulation [52]. Following the competition, a broader range of approaches have been investigated. Rajabi, et al. [53] proposed a method based on a Conditional Deep Convolutional Generative Adversarial Network (CDCGAN) and the Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM) for real-time leak detection and localization on L-Town, achieving approximately 70% detection accuracy, while Wang, et al. [54] developed a hydraulic-model-free approach based on graph theory and Bayesian reasoning, with localization errors below 300 m for all 10 leak points and below 70 m in some cases. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\ndblp: Gabriele Restuccia (https://dblp.org/pid/279/3951.html)\nciteturn10search5 [wordlim: 200] Published: 7 months ago; Crawled: 5 months ago; Centrality-Aware Machine Learning for Water Network Pressure Prediction.\n    * export record\n\n      * BibTeX\n      * RIS\n      * RDF N-Triples\n      * RDF Turtle\n      * RDF/XML\n      * XML\n      * plain text\n\ndblp key:\n\n      * conf/networking/AmatoPRT25\n\n    * ask others\n\n      * Google\n      * Google Scholar\n      * Semantic Scholar\n      * Internet Archive Scholar\n      * CiteSeerX\n      * PubPeer\n\n    * share record\n\n      * Bluesky\n      * Reddit\n      * BibSonomy\n      * LinkedIn\n\npersistent URL:\n\n      * https://dblp.org/rec/conf/networking/AmatoPRT25\n\nFederico Amato, Antonino Pagano, Gabriele Restuccia, Ilenia Tinnirello:\nCentrality-Aware Machine Learning for Water Network Pressure Prediction. CONCLUSIONS \nThis study compared four machine learning algorithms: \nAutoencoder LSTM, Isolation Forest, One-Class SVM, and \nKNN, for leak detection in water distribution networks with \nlimited infrastructure, such as Huancayo, Peru, where losses \naccount for 32.82% of the distributed water. Using a historical \nconsumption dataset structured in 12-month sequences per \nclient, key metrics such as F1 Score, Precision, and MAE were \nevaluated. The Autoencoder LSTM achieved the highest \nPrecision \n(0.89) \nand \nthe \nlowest \nMAE \n(0.00402), \ndemonstrating stability and low error rates. Although the One-\nClass SVM obtained the highest F1 Score (0.4452), its high \nMAE (0.50129) revealed less consistency in predictions. \nThese findings demonstrate that the Autoencoder LSTM \nmodel is a reliable tool for managing water losses, enabling \nearly interventions for potential leaks and reducing \noperational burdens with high precision and robustness. \nDetection and Localization Using Hydraulic Modeling and\nClassification,\" Journal of Hydroinformatics, vol. 23, no. 4, pp. 782–\n799, 2021. Leveraging the inherent graph structure of WDNs, recent approaches have used graph-based data-driven methods. However, these methods often learn shortcuts that work well with in-distribution data but fail to generalize to out-of-distribution data.\nL17: To address this limitation and inspired by the perfect generalization ability of classical algorithms, we propose an algorithm-informed graph neural network (AIGNN). Recognizing that WDNs function as flow networks, incorporating max-flow information can be beneficial for inferring pressures. In the proposed framework, we first train AIGNN to emulate the Ford-Fulkerson algorithm for solving max-flow problems. This algorithmic knowledge is then transferred to address the pressure estimation problem in WDNs.\nL18: Two AIGNNs are deployed, one to reconstruct pressure based on the current measurements, and another to predict pressure based on previous measurements. Leakages are detected and localized by comparing the outputs of the reconstructor and the predictor. cite6†CIRSEAU L31:   * cite7†Contact L32: Publication: The performance of encoder–decoder neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks\nL33: \nL34:   * Number of downloads 28\nL35:   * File size 975.65 KB\nL36:   * Upload-Date 7. August 2024\nL37:   * cite8†Download L38: # Publication: The performance of encoder–decoder neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks\nL39: This work outlines the performance of three variants of deep neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks, namely – autoencoders (AEs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), and long short-term memory autoencoders (LSTM-AEs). The multivariate pressure signals reconstructed from these models are analysed for leakage identification. The leak onset time is estimated using a fast approximation sliding window technique, which computes statistical discrepancies in prediction errors.\nL40: The performance of all three variants is validated\nL41: using the widely studied L-Town ben hmark network. Furthermore, their feasibility for real-world application is studied by applying them to a real-world case study representing the data availability and network design often found in smaller- and medium-sized utilities in Norway. The results for the benchmark network showed that AE and LSTM-AE showed comparable detection performance for abrupt leaks with VAE performing the least.\nL42: For incipient leaks, the LSTM-AE showed better detection performance with few false-positives. For the real-world dataset, the performance was significantly lower due to the quantity and quality of data available, and the contradiction of inherent requirements of data-driven models. In addition, the analysis revealed that the positioning of pressure sensors in the network is critical for the leak detection performance of these models.\nL43: cite9†Zimmermann 2024-08-07T08:33:40+02:00\nL44: \nL45: cite10†Image: EU flag L46: \nL47: This project has received funding from the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under grant agreement No. 869171.\nL48: \nL49: cite10†Image: EU flag L50: \nL51: © Copyright | B-WaterSmart\nL52: \nL53:   * cite11†Legal Disclaimer L54:   * cite12†Privacy Policy L55:   * cite13†Terms and Acronyms L56: # Get project news\nL57: \nL58: * * *\nL59: \nL60: [Input] [Input]\nL61: \nL62: First name or full name [Input]\nL63: \nL64: Email  (is required) [Input]\nL65: \nL66: \nL67: \nL68: [Input]  By continuing, you accept our cite12†privacy policy .\nL69: \nL70: [Input]\nL71: \nL72: cite8†Page load link "

August 2024\nL37:   * cite8†Download L38: # Publication: The performance of encoder–decoder neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks\nL39: This work outlines the performance of three variants of deep neural networks for leak detection in water distribution networks, namely – autoencoders (AEs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), and long short-term memory autoencoders (LSTM-AEs). The multivariate pressure signals reconstructed from these models are analysed for leakage identification. The leak onset time is estimated using a fast approximation sliding window technique, which computes statistical discrepancies in prediction errors.\nL40: The performance of all three variants is validated\nL41: using the widely studied L-Town ben hmark network. Furthermore, their feasibility for real-world application is studied by applying them to a real-world case study representing the data availability and network design often found in smaller- and medium-sized utilities in Norway. The results for the benchmark network showed that AE and LSTM-AE showed comparable detection performance for abrupt leaks with VAE performing the least.\nL42: For incipient leaks, the LSTM-AE showed better detection performance with few false-positives. For the real-world dataset, the performance was significantly lower due to the quantity and quality of data available, and the contradiction of inherent requirements of data-driven models. In addition, the analysis revealed that the positioning of pressure sensors in the network is critical for the leak detection performance of these models.\nL43: cite9†Zimmermann 2024-08-07T08:33:40+02:00\nL44: \nL45: cite10†Image: EU flag L46: \nL47: This project has received funding from the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under grant agreement No. 869171.\nL48: \nL49: cite10†Image: EU flag L50: \nL51: © Copyright | B-WaterSmart\nL52: \nL53:   * cite11†Legal Disclaimer L54:   * cite12†Privacy Policy L55:   * cite13†Terms and Acronyms L56: # Get project news\nL57: \nL58: * * *\nL59: \nL60: [Input] [Input]\nL61: \nL62: First name or full name [Input]\nL63: \nL64: Email  (is required) [Input]\nL65: \nL66: \nL67: \nL68: [Input]  By continuing, you accept our cite12†privacy policy .\nL69: \nL70: [Input]\nL71: \nL72: cite8†Page load link "

(2020), shown in Figure 3. The network has recently been studied by several researchers for several modelling studies (e.g., Blocher et al. 2020; Daniel et al. 2022; Tornyeviadzi & Seidu 2023), and has been the core benchmark for leakage detection and isolation methods (BattLeDIM) competition in 2020. The network has three zones, 905 pipes, and 782 nodes, supplied from two reservoirs and a tank. The model consists of different leaks occurring at different time instants in the years 2018 and 2019. Measurements from 33 pressure sensors, 10 flow meters and 84 smart water meters are available. For the reported study, only pressure measurements (see Figure 3) located at different parts of the network are used for the current analysis.\n\nFigure 3\n\nView largeDownload slide\n\nL-Town BattleDIM network showing the locations of pressure sensors and leaks.\n\nFigure 3\n\nView largeDownload slide\n\nL-Town BattleDIM network showing the locations of pressure sensors and leaks.\n\nClose Figure 3\n\n### Dataset generation\n\nListen\n\nAs described earlier, the study builds on semi-supervised learning wherein, measurements from leak-free scenarios are used for identifying new leaks without explicitly using historical leaks as labels for training encoder–decoder models. In the first step, the nominal hydraulic model without leaks is simulated using the Water Network Tool for Resilience (WNTR) Python toolkit (Klise et al. 2017), and the pressures at all junctions with sensors is stored for the years 2018 and 2019. The pressure measurements are then split into train-validation datasets in an 80–20 ratio. The training and validation data are z-standardised for zero mean and unit variance to enable efficient model training. ## RESULTS AND DISCUSSION\n\nListen\n\n### Case 1: L-Town WDN\n\nListen\n\nIdeally, when there is no leak, the reconstruction error timeseries should be zero except for noise in measurements and due to model prediction error. When a leak occurs, the reconstruction error increases above the noise threshold, thereby indicating the onset of the leak. Hence, the pressure sensor closest to the leak position will have the highest reconstruction error. As an illustration, the error series for an abrupt leak (p142) and an incipient leak (p653) are shown in Figure 6.\n\nFigure 6\n\nView largeDownload slide\n\nSudden and gradual changes in reconstruction errors for abrupt leak p142 (Top) and incipient leak p653 (bottom) for L-Town.\n\nFigure 6\n\nView largeDownload slide\n\nSudden and gradual changes in reconstruction errors for abrupt leak p142 (Top) and incipient leak p653 (bottom) for L-Town.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nReconstruction of hydraulic states in water distribution systems via radial basis function neural networks | Bollettino dell'Unione Matematica Italiana | Springer Nature Link (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40574-026-00529-3)\nciteturn13search6 [wordlim: 200] Published: 7 months ago; Crawled: last month; The dataset is based on the hypothetical urban scenario of L-Town, which features a meshed WDN with a total pipe length of 42.6 km, divided into three operational zones. ... Instrumentation of the network includes one tank-level sensor, two flow sensors, and 33 pressure sensors.\n## 3 Proposed method\n\nWe used two Water Distribution Networks (WDNs) to test our RBF-NN model. The first is L-Town, shown in Fig. 1, whose characteristics are illustrate in Table 1. The second model was Northwest System, whose layout is in Fig. 3 and Table 4.\n\nIn particular, one of the Water Distribution Network (WDN) analyzed in this study is the one adopted in the BattLeDIM challenge—Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods [23]. The dataset is based on the hypothetical urban scenario of L-Town, which features a meshed WDN with a total pipe length of 42.6 km, divided into three operational zones. The network comprises 782 nodes and 909 pipes.\n\nInstrumentation of the network includes one tank-level sensor, two flow sensors, and 33 pressure sensors. These devices continuously monitor hydraulic states over a one-week period, providing data snapshots at 5-minute intervals. A summary of the network and sensor configuration is reported in Table 1, while the network topology is illustrated in Fig. 1.\n\nFig. 1\n\nImage: Fig. 1\n\nFull size image\n\nTopological layout of the Water Distribution Network (L-Town)\n\nTable 1 Water distribution network components and monitoring details for L-Town\n\nThe main objective of this work is to develop an interpolative model capable of reconstructing pressure and flow fields across the entire Water Distribution Network (WDN), using only the information provided by a limited set of pressure and flow sensors. One of the key challenges lies in extrapolating from sparse, localized measurements to a full-network hydraulic state, particularly in a system that is spatially distributed and topologically irregular.\n\nTo address this, we implemented a feedforward neural network architecture with radial basis function (RBF) activations in the hidden layer. Although often referred to as a Radial Basis Function Neural Network (RBFNN), the model differs from the classical formulation by enabling full-state inference at a fixed time instant through a single forward pass, rather than localized interpolation based on distance to fixed centers.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n(PDF) Interpretable Event Diagnosis in Water Distribution Networks (https://www.researchgate.net/publication/391676477_Interpretable_Event_Diagnosis_in_Water_Distribution_Networks)\nciteturn13search7 [wordlim: 200] Published: 1.4 years ago; Crawled: 5 months ago; Sensor Fault 0.94 0.93 0.94 ... Case Study II\n\nWe consider a variant of the realistic L-Town water distribution network [45] – we demon-\n\nstrate our approach on the hydraulically isolated Area A (see Fig. 5), by closing the valves\n\nthat provide water to Area B and C. Area A consists of 661 nodes and 766 links. The\n\nbenchmark assumes the existence of 29 pressure sensors at nodes that have been optimally\n\n16\n\nplaced to maximize the detectability of leakages.\n\nFigure 5: The L-Town network (Area A) (Vrachimis et al., 2022) and the locations of 29 pressure sensors.\n\n10.1. Quantitative Performance Evaluation\n\nWe use two diﬀerent instances of L-Town networks: One instance with artiﬁcial demand\n\npatterns – this instance is supposed to model the modeled network by the water utility –,\n\nand an instance with (noisy) realistic demand patterns and changed hydraulic dynamics,\n\nwhich is achieved by adding random noise to the realistic demand patterns and closing some\n\nvalves to change the dynamics – this instance is supposed to model the real world which is\n\na bit diﬀerent from the model used by the water utility.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n(PDF) A Multivariate Statistical Framework for Detection, Classification and Pre-localization of Anomalies in Water Distribution Networks (https://www.researchgate.net/publication/398806238_A_Multivariate_Statistical_Framework_for_Detection_Classification_and_Pre-localization_of_Anomalies_in_Water_Distribution_Networks)\nciteturn13search8 [wordlim: 200] Published: 9 months ago; Crawled: last month; L-Town model evaluated at 2018-01-01 00:00 and were constructed by adding ... sure sensor n215), and area C (pressure sensors n1, n4, and n31).\n\nAnother distinctive feature of the data is the strong spatial correlation\n\nbetween the monitored parameters (pressure, water ﬂow rate), illustrated in\n\nFig. 4.
